I know it's probably not normal to lose control like that... is that helpful for you to know?\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Um... well, honestly, I feel pretty awful. Just really guilty, I guess? So I always try to... make up for it, if that makes sense. Like, I want to fix it so I can be good again. \n\nSo after one of those nights, I’ll tell myself I’m just not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school the next day. Which, you know, is usually fine because I'm so busy with the kids anyway... but then after work, I try to be really good about getting some exercise in to balance things out. I'll stay out on the trail for hours, or I'll force myself to do extra yoga... even if I'm feeling really shaky and just completely exhausted. \n\nI just feel like I have to undo it, you know? Is... is that the right kind of thing to tell you? I hope I'm not sounding too crazy, I just want to make sure I'm giving you the information you need.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food happen?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it's been going on for a little while now. I'd say for the past few months, honestly. And... well, I guess it happens pretty often? \n\nI mean, there's rarely a week that goes by without at least one night where I... you know, eat way more than I meant to. And then, of course, the next day I have to do all that stuff I mentioned—skipping meals at school or making sure I go on a really long walk or do extra yoga to make up for it. \n\nI know it's probably not the best routine, and I'm really trying to be better about it, I promise. I just... I get so overwhelmed. Is that... does that sound like a lot to you? I hope I'm giving you the right timeline, I don't want to make it sound worse than it is, but I also want to be honest with you.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No, I would never do that. I know some people do, but... that just seems so extreme, you know? \n\nI really just try to handle it in a healthy way, like I said, with the fasting and the exercise. I mean, I know skipping meals isn't perfect, but it's much better than... well, than throwing up, right? I try really hard to keep things somewhat normal. Does that make sense? I hope that's a good thing, that I don't do that.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Um... yes. Yes, I am. Honestly, it's... it's something I think about a lot. Probably more than I should. \n\nI just... I want to look professional and put-together, you know? But sometimes it feels like it takes over everything. Like, if I look even a little softer around my stomach, or... or if my pants feel even a tiny bit tight before a staff meeting, I just... I honestly feel like I've completely failed. At everything. \n\nIt doesn't even matter if my students are doing really well, or if I spent hours preparing the perfect lesson plan. If I feel like I've gained weight, all of that just goes out the window, and I feel awful about myself. \n\nIs... is it normal to care that much? Um... yeah. I guess I kind of brought that up earlier, didn't I? But... yes, it really does.

I just... I get so overwhelmed. Is that... does that sound like a lot to you? I hope I'm giving you the right timeline, I don't want to make it sound worse than it is, but I also want to be honest with you.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. I mean, I know skipping meals isn't perfect, but it's much better than... well, than throwing up, right? I try really hard to keep things somewhat normal. Does that make sense? I hope that's a good thing, that I don't do that.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Um... yes. Yes, I am. Honestly, it's... it's something I think about a lot. No, I would never do that. I know some people do, but... that just seems so extreme, you know? \n\nI really just try to handle it in a healthy way, like I said, with the fasting and the exercise. I mean, I know skipping meals isn't perfect, but it's much better than... well, than throwing up, right? I try really hard to keep things somewhat normal. Does that make sense? If I feel like I've gained weight, all of that just goes out the window, and I feel awful about myself. \n\nIs... is it normal to care that much? I'm trying to have a better perspective, I really am.\n\nDoctor: How often do you engage in these activities like extra exercise or skipping meals to make up for the episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it's... it's pretty much tied to whenever I have one of those nights where I eat too much. I want to make sure I'm fixing my mistakes right away. That's... that's an okay way to handle it, right? I mean, since I'm not doing anything extreme, it's just trying to be responsible and get back on track. I feel like I have to balance it out, you know? So it happens about as often as the eating does—at least once a week, maybe a little more if it was a particularly bad week. \n\nI just... I want to make sure I'm fixing my mistakes right away. That's... that's an okay way to handle it, right? I mean, since I'm not doing anything extreme, it's just trying to be responsible and get back on track. I would never mess with medications or anything like that. I mean, taking pills to fix it just seems like it would be crossing a line, right? I guess I kind of brought that up earlier, didn't I? I could have spent all weekend preparing this amazing lesson plan, and my students could be having the best day ever, but if my clothes don't fit right, all of that just... disappears, and I feel terrible about myself. \n\nI know I shouldn't let it get to me that much, and I'm really trying to work on having a healthier mindset about it. It's just normal insecurity, though, right? I mean, most women struggle with their body image and let it affect their confidence at least a little bit, don't they? People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, yes... I mean, mostly, yes. I just... I really don't want to gain weight. I know that probably sounds a little vain, and I'm sorry if it does. I just want to stay fit and, you know, look presentable for work. 

So, after one of those nights where I eat too much, I'll tell myself that I'm just not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school the next day. And then after work, I'll stay out on the trail for hours, or I'll do extra yoga... sometimes even when I'm feeling really shaky and exhausted. I just push through it because I feel like I have to burn it all off so I don't put on any weight. 

I really hope you don't think I'm being foolish or extreme for doing that... it's just my way of trying to fix my mistakes and keep things under control. That makes sense, doesn't it? I just want to make sure I'm explaining myself the right way.

I just... I get so overwhelmed. Is that... does that sound like a lot to you? No, I would never do that. I know some people do, but... that just seems so extreme, you know? \n\nI really just try to handle it in a healthy way, like I said, with the fasting and the exercise. I mean, I know skipping meals isn't perfect, but it's much better than... well, than throwing up, right? I try really hard to keep things somewhat normal. Does that make sense? I hope that's a good thing, that I don't do that.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Um... yes. Yes, I am. Honestly, it's... it's something I think about a lot. At everything. \n\nIt doesn't even matter if my students are doing really well, or if I spent hours preparing the perfect lesson plan. If I feel like I've gained weight, all of that just goes out the window, and I feel awful about myself. \n\nIs... is it normal to care that much? I'm trying to have a better perspective, I really am.\n\nDoctor: How often do you engage in these activities like extra exercise or skipping meals to make up for the episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it's... it's pretty much tied to whenever I have one of those nights where I eat too much. So, like I mentioned earlier, for the past few months, it's been at least once a week. \n\nWhenever I have a night where I slip up and eat more than I meant to, the very next day I make sure to do the extra yoga or the long walk, or I just skip lunch at school. I feel like I have to balance it out, you know? So it happens about as often as the eating does—at least once a week, maybe a little more if it was a particularly bad week. \n\nI just... I want to make sure I'm fixing my mistakes right away. That's... that's an okay way to handle it, right? I mean, since I'm not doing anything extreme, it's just trying to be responsible and get back on track. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to undo the effects of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no. No, definitely not. I... I would never mess with medications or anything like that. That just sounds really dangerous, don't you think? \n\nI know some people use things like that, but I'm really careful about what I put in my body. I just stick to the natural things, like the walking and the yoga, and just being careful the next day. I really want to be healthy about all of this. I mean, taking pills to fix it just seems like it would be crossing a line, right? I'm doing the right thing by staying away from all that, aren't I?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape affects how you feel about yourself as a person?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah. I guess I kind of brought that up earlier, didn't I? But... yes, it really does. \n\nLike I was saying before, if I wake up and notice I look even a little softer around my middle, or if my work pants feel just a tiny bit tight right before a staff meeting... I honestly just feel like I've failed. At everything. It completely overshadows all the good things I do. I could have spent all weekend preparing this amazing lesson plan, and my students could be having the best day ever, but if my clothes don't fit right, all of that just... disappears, and I feel terrible about myself. \n\nI know I shouldn't let it get to me that much, and I'm really trying to work on having a healthier mindset about it. It's just normal insecurity, though, right? I mean, most women struggle with their body image and let it affect their confidence at least a little bit, don't they? I just want to make sure I'm not totally crazy for feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your self-worth is largely tied to how you perceive your body shape and weight?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah... I suppose that's exactly what it comes down to. Like I was just saying, it really does dictate how I see myself. If my stomach looks even a little softer, or if those pants feel tight before a staff meeting... it just completely takes over. It doesn't matter how well my students are doing or how prepared I am for the day, I just honestly feel like I've failed at everything. \n\nBut... I mean, I know deep down that my actual worth as a person isn't *only* about my weight. I know I'm a good teacher! It's just really hard to separate the two in those moments. I'm really trying to be more positive and focus on the good things, I promise. It's just a confidence thing, which is pretty normal to struggle with, right? I'm sure a lot of people tie their self-esteem to how they look at least a little bit... don't they?\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in these compensatory activities, like extra exercise or skipping meals, every time you have an episode of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Um... well, I guess, yeah, pretty much every time. I hope that doesn't sound too bad, I just... I really like to keep things balanced. \n\nIf I'm being completely honest, for the past few months, there has rarely been a week without at least one night where I end up eating far more than I meant to. And when that happens, I just feel like I need to fix it. So the next day, I try to make up for it by not eating at school, or I'll make sure I push myself through a really long walk or some extra yoga when I get home. \n\nIt's just my way of resetting and staying on track, you know? Like, if you make a mistake, you try to correct it. That's a pretty normal way to handle a slip-up, isn't it? I'm just trying to be responsible and take care of myself after I overdo it... I think that's the right thing to do, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you always skip meals or exercise after eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, I guess I do. I know I just mentioned this, but... yeah, pretty much always. \n\nLike I was saying, for the last few months, it's been... well, almost every week there's at least one night where I just eat way more than I planned to. So I'll just skip lunch at school, or I'll make sure I do a really long walk or some extra yoga after work. \n\nI just... I really want to be healthy, you know? And if I mess up, I just want to fix it right away so it doesn't get out of hand. That makes sense, right? Like, it's good that I'm trying to correct my mistakes and stay active... isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in these compensatory activities, like extra exercise or skipping meals, after every single episode of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I'm sorry, did I not explain that right a second ago? I just try to be really disciplined about making up for it so I don't let myself go. Is that... is that what you were asking? I hope that doesn't sound too out of control... I mean, I always make sure to fix it right away, so it's not like I'm just letting myself go completely. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... yeah, I guess I do. I mean, I really try to control it, I promise I do. I'm usually such an organized, put-together person, especially for my students. But... 

When I have one of those nights, it's like... once I start, it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, you know? Like she's standing there saying, "Joan, stop, you don't need this," but... my hands just keep opening things. I just keep putting food in my mouth until I'm so painfully full I can barely move. 

I'm so sorry, I know that sounds terrible and completely out of control. I really don't want you to think I'm just this weak person who can't stop eating. It's just in those moments... it feels like I can't stop. But I do try my best to be good the rest of the time. That counts for something, doesn't it?

So, like I mentioned earlier, for the past few months, it's been at least once a week. \n\nWhenever I have a night where I slip up and eat more than I meant to, the very next day I make sure to do the extra yoga or the long walk, or I just skip lunch at school. I feel like I have to balance it out, you know? So it happens about as often as the eating does—at least once a week, maybe a little more if it was a particularly bad week. \n\nI just... I want to make sure I'm fixing my mistakes right away. That's... that's an okay way to handle it, right? I mean, since I'm not doing anything extreme, it's just trying to be responsible and get back on track. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to undo the effects of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no. No, definitely not. I... I would never mess with medications or anything like that. I'm sure a lot of people tie their self-esteem to how they look at least a little bit... don't they?\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in these compensatory activities, like extra exercise or skipping meals, every time you have an episode of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Um... well, I guess, yeah, pretty much every time. Like, if you make a mistake, you try to correct it. That's a pretty normal way to handle a slip-up, isn't it? I'm just trying to be responsible and take care of myself after I overdo it... I think that's the right thing to do, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you always skip meals or exercise after eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, I guess I do. I know I just mentioned this, but... yeah, pretty much always. \n\nLike I was saying, for the last few months, it's been... well, almost every week there's at least one night where I just eat way more than I planned to. And if I mess up, I just want to fix it right away so it doesn't get out of hand. That makes sense, right? Like, it's good that I'm trying to correct my mistakes and stay active... isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in these compensatory activities, like extra exercise or skipping meals, after every single episode of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... That shows I'm still trying to be responsible, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have a week where you don't engage in extra exercise or skip meals after eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Um, no... I don't think so. I'm sorry, I know we just talked about this, and I really don't want to sound like a broken record or like I'm not listening to you. \n\nBut, um, for the past few months, there really hasn't been a week that goes by without at least one night where I just eat way more than I meant to. And so, because of that, there's never a week where I don't try to make up for it the next day. I always end up skipping lunch at school or pushing myself to do a really long walk or extra yoga. \n\nI just... I can't let it sit there, you know? I have to fix it. I know I just mentioned this, but... yeah, pretty much always. \n\nLike I was saying, for the last few months, it's been... well, almost every week there's at least one night where I just eat way more than I planned to. And when that happens, I just... I really feel like I have to balance it out the next day. So I'll just skip lunch at school, or I'll make sure I do a really long walk or some extra yoga after work. \n\nI just... I really want to be healthy, you know? And if I mess up, I just want to fix it right away so it doesn't get out of hand. That makes sense, right? Like, it's good that I'm trying to correct my mistakes and stay active... isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in these compensatory activities, like extra exercise or skipping meals, after every single episode of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I just try to be really disciplined about making up for it so I don't let myself go. Is that... is that what you were asking? I just want to make sure I'm doing this right.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month would you say you have one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Um, well... There's usually at least one night a week where I just... I end up eating a lot more than I intended. That shows I'm still trying to be responsible, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have a week where you don't engage in extra exercise or skip meals after eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Um, no...
